## **3.1.3 System Security and Algorithmic Design**

	The creation of Katuwang, a web-based platform that serves secondary students including minors, requires thoughtful and well-founded choices regarding system security and fundamental algorithmic design. In addition to choosing a technology stack, the research team assessed particular implementation decisions that have an immediate impact on the platform's efficacy, safety and integrity. This section explains the reasoning behind three crucial design choices: using a weighted scoring algorithm to match student learners with the best peer tutor, adopting Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) as the access management model to enforce user permissions, and choosing bcrypt as the password hashing algorithm to secure user credentials. In order to ensure that the selected strategies are not only technically sound but also proportionate to the operational scale resource limitations and data privacy obligations of a school-based platform serving the Taysan Resettlement Integrated School community, each decision was made by comparing the available alternatives across pertinent criteria.

### **3.1.3.1 Password Hashing Using Bcrypt** 

A crucial security precaution is password hashing, which transforms plaintext passwords into an unchangeable, fixed-length string of characters. Hashed passwords are much safer to store in a database than encryption, since they cannot be decrypted (Grassi et al., 2017). Because of its design features, which are especially well suited for password security in web-based applications, bcrypt was chosen for Katuwang among the available hashing algorithms.

By adding a randomly generated salt to each password prior to hashing, bcrypt is an adaptive hashing function that stops attackers from cracking stored passwords using precomputed hash tables, also referred to as rainbow tables. More significantly, bcrypt has an adjustable cost factor that regulates the computational cost of the hashing procedure. Because the cost factor can be raised to prevent brute-force attacks, bcrypt is intrinsically future-resistant by design (Grassi et al., 2017). According to Aumasson (2018), bcrypt is far more resistant to contemporary attack vectors than general-purpose hashing functions because of its intentional slowness and salting mechanism. 

Faster hashing algorithms like MD5 and SHA-256, on the other hand, were created for data integrity verification and speed rather than password storage. Because of their computational efficiency, they are susceptible to dictionary and brute-force attacks, because modern hardware allows an attacker to try billions of guesses every second (Grassi et al., 2017). Although SHA-3 is more secure than MD5 and SHA-256, it still lacks the native adaptive cost factor and built-in salting that bcrypt offers (Dworkin et al., 2015). The 2015 Password Hashing Competition winner, Argon2, is currently regarded as the most theoretically sound choice and provides memory-hard defense against GPU-based attacks (Biryukov et al., 2016); however, for a school-based platform functioning at the scale of TRIS, it necessitates extra configuration and dependency management, which increases implementation complexity without offering much practical benefit. The comparison of these hashing algorithms is summed up in Table 4\.

# 

**Table 4:** *Comparison of Password Hashing Algorithms*

| Algorithm | Built-in Salting | Adaptive Cost Factor | Hashing Speed | Brute force Resistance | Implementation Complexity | Suitability for Password Storage |
| ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| MD5 | No | No | Very fast | Very low | Low | Not recommended |
| SHA-256 | No | No | Very fast | Low | Low | Not recommended |
| SHA-3 | No | No | Fast | Moderate | Low | Acceptable with manual salting |
| **Bcrypt (selected)** | **Yes** | **Yes** | **Intentionally slow** | **High** | **Low–Moderate** | **Highly recommended** |
| Argon2 | Yes | Yes | Intentionally slow | Very high | Moderate-High | Highly recommended |

As demonstrated in Table 4, bcrypt continues to be the industry standard for web applications because of its native salting, configurable cost factor, broad library support, and low implementation complexity, all of which are in line with Katuwang's technical scope and resource constraints, despite Argon2's potential advantage in fending off GPU-based attacks.

### **3.1.3.2 Role-Based Access Control for User Permissions** 

A security model called Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) limits system access according to the roles that each user is assigned. Rather than giving each user direct access to permissions, RBAC links permissions to roles. Users are then given a role that matches their role in the system. This lowers the possibility of illegal access, data breaches, and unintentional abuse of system functions by guaranteeing that users can only access features and information pertinent to their duties (Hu et al., 2018).

There are three different user roles in Katuwang: Administrator, Student Tutor, and Student Learner. Depending on its purpose, each role has a unique set of permissions. For instance, student learners are able to take tests and request tutoring, but they are unable to view user profiles or access the analytics dashboard. Although they can access all system management tools, administrators are unable to change the session records of other users without the required authorization. RBAC successfully operationalizes this fundamental tenet of secure system design in multi-user web applications, the principle of least privilege, according to which each user is given only the minimal access required to carry out their role (Servos and Osborn, 2017). 

Because RBAC offers a framework for a multi-role educational platform that is organized, controllable, and auditable, it was chosen over other access control models. Since permissions must be set up per user per resource, the Discretionary Access Control (DAC) and Access Control List (ACL) models become unmanageable as the number of users increases (Servos and Osborn, 2017). Despite being extremely secure, Mandatory Access Control (MAC) is too strict for a voluntary school-based platform and is intended for classified government or military settings (Santos et al., 2020). Although powerful and adaptable, Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) adds needless policy complexity to a system with just three distinct user types. RBAC is easy to implement with NextAuth.JS; session tokens are simple to audit and correspond directly to Katuwang's three pre-established user roles. The comparison of these access control models is compiled in Table 5\.

**Table 5:** *Comparison of Access Control Models*

| Access Control Model | Permission Assignment | Scalability | Ease of Management | Auditability | Suitable for Multi-role Systems | Suitability for Katuwang |
| ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Discretionary Access Control (DAC) | Owner-defined per resource | Low | Difficult | Low | No | Not suitable |
| Mandatory Access Control (MAC) | System-enforced security labels | Moderate | Complex | High | Partially | Not suitable — overly rigid |
| Access Control List (ACL) | Per-user, per-resource rules | Low | Difficult at scale | Moderate | No | Not suitable |
| Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) | Policies based on user/resource attributes | High | Complex | High | Yes | Overkill for current scope |
| **Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) (selected)** | **Permissions assigned to roles; users assigned to roles** | **High** | **Simple** | **High** | **Yes** | **Highly suitable** |

# 

Given its simplicity, scalability, and direct alignment with the platform's well-defined user role structure, RBAC is the best access control model for Katuwang, as Table 5 illustrates. 

### **3.1.3.3 Weighted Scoring Algorithm for Tutor Matching** 

A weighted scoring algorithm is a decision-making method that evaluates multiple criteria simultaneously by assigning a numerical weight to each criterion based on its relative importance, computing a score for each candidate against those criteria, and selecting the candidate with the highest total weighted score (Zavadskas et al., 2016). In the context of tutor matching, this approach ensures that the most relevant factors such as whether the tutor has demonstrated proficiency in the subject the learner needs help with, whether the tutor and learner are at compatible grade levels, and whether both parties are available at the same time are all considered together rather than in isolation, producing a match that is both academically appropriate and logistically feasible. 

Since the weighted scoring algorithm is transparent, adaptable, and computationally light, it was chosen over alternative matching techniques. In contrast to black-box machine learning models, which necessitate substantial computational resources and large training datasets (Jordan and Mitchell, 2015), a weighted scoring algorithm uses pre-established, human-interpretable rules that administrators can modify as TRIS requirements change. Weighted scoring creates an ordered list of candidates so that the best match is always given priority, in contrast to straightforward rule-based filtering that just removes unqualified tutors without ranking them (Zavadskas et al., 2016). Although collaborative filtering works well in large-scale recommendation systems, it needs a lot of historical interaction data to produce accurate recommendations; data that a recently implemented platform that serves a single school does not yet have (Zhang et al., 2019). Constraint-based matching produces matches that are technically valid but may not be ideal, because it assesses hard requirements without having the flexibility to prioritize some criteria over others (Zhang et al., 2019). All of these drawbacks are addressed by the weighted scoring algorithm, which evaluates all pertinent criteria concurrently, generates a ranked list of qualified tutors, doesn't require training data, is computationally light enough to operate on modest school infrastructure, and lets administrators modify the weight of each criterion as the program develops. The comparison of these matching approaches is summarized in Table 6\.

### 

**Table 6:** *Comparisons of Matching Approaches*

| Approach | Multi-Criteria | Ranked Results | Transparency | Training Data Required | Computational Load | Reconfigurable | Suitability for Katuwang |
| ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Manual Matching (Admin-assigned) | Partially | No | High | No | None | Yes | Not scalable |
| Simple Rule-Based Filtering | Partially | No | High | No | Very low | Yes | Acceptable but limited |
| Collaborative Filtering | Yes | Yes | Low | Yes, large dataset | High | No | Not suitable  insufficient data |
| Machine Learning-Based Matching | Yes | Yes | Low | Yes, extensive dataset | High | No | Not suitable  overkill for scope |
| Constraint-Based Matching | Yes | Yes | High | No | Low | Yes | Acceptable but inflexible |
| **Weighted Scoring Algorithm (selected)** | **Yes** | **Yes** | **High** | **No** | **Very low** | **Yes** | **Highly suitable** |

Given its transparency, adaptability, low computational requirements, and compatibility with the operational limitations of a recently implemented school-based platform, the weighted scoring algorithm is the best matching strategy for Katuwang, as indicated in Table 6\. 

### **3.1.3.4 Displayed Name Generation Policy** 

To protect the identity of student users while maintaining a structured and traceable platform environment, Katuwang will implement an automatic displayed name generation system for all registered users. Rather than allowing users to freely select their own displayed names  which introduces risks of impersonation, inappropriate content, and identity duplication. The platform will assign a system-generated unique identifier to each user upon successful registration. This will align with the platform's data minimization principle under the Data Privacy Act of 2012 (Republic Act No. 10173), ensuring that no personally identifiable information such as a student's real name or school ID number will be exposed within the platform interface. 

The generated identifier will follow a role-based prefix format, where Student Tutors will be assigned identifiers following the format TUT-XXXX and Student Learners will be assigned identifiers following the format STU-XXXX, with XXXX representing a four-digit zero-padded sequential number. The first registered tutor will receive TUT-0001, the second TUT-0002, and so on, independently from the learner sequence which will begin at STU-0001. This format will provide immediate role recognition without disclosing any personal information about the user. 

This identifier will be generated through a simple sequential counter algorithm which would be unique for each registered user. Upon the success of registering a new user, the system would deduce the users role and append the appropriate prefix, also consulting another sequence counter table. This table would contain the last number assigned to each prefix independently. This value will be incremented, padded with leading zero until the length of the number is four, concatenated with the prefix and then checked for uniqueness against the users table. If the identifier already exists then the process will be repeated by incrementing the counter and continuing until a unique identifier is found and assigned to the user profile. 

# **3.2 Software Methodology**

The researchers will adopt the Agile Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC) using the Scrum framework to guide the development of Katuwang. Agile Scrum was selected for its flexibility, iterative approach, and strong emphasis on continuous feedback. According to Schwaber and Sutherland (2020) in the Official Scrum Guide. Scrum is a lightweight framework designed to help teams generate value through adaptive solutions for complex problems, making it especially suitable for projects whose requirements evolve through stakeholder engagement. This flexibility allows the research team to refine and improve the platform at any stage before final delivery, which is particularly important for Katuwang, as the specific academic support needs of Taysan Resettlement Integrated School can only be fully understood through ongoing consultations with the school principal, selected faculty, and selected students. Furthermore, Agile's iterative structure, organized into short development cycles or sprints, enables the team to deliver functional system increments regularly rather than waiting for full completion before presenting results to stakeholders (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). This approach allows the principal and selected faculty of TRIS to review progress and provide timely feedback, ensuring that the platform consistently reflects the actual academic needs of the school community.

The Agile Scrum process followed in this study is illustrated in Figure 2, which depicts the cyclical flow from Requirements Planning and User Design through the Product Backlog, Sprint Planning, Daily Scrum, Sprint Release, Retrospective, and Refinement, all supported by continuous feedback loops from the school principal, selected faculty, and selected students of Taysan Resettlement Integrated School.  

***Figure 2\.** Agile Process Flow (Scrum.org, 2020\)*

### **3.2.1 Requirements Planning**

The first phase of the development process will focus on the systematic gathering, analysis, and documentation of the system requirements that Katuwang must fulfill. This phase will combine a review of related literature and existing peer tutoring platforms with direct stakeholder consultation at TRIS, including the school principal, selected faculty members, and selected students. Data will be collected through semi-structured interviews and a needs assessment survey, ensuring that the identified requirements accurately reflect the actual academic support workflow of the school, including the voluntary and free-of-charge nature of the peer tutoring services and the specific subject areas and grade levels that require intervention. All gathered requirements will be organized into a structured list of system features and constraints, clearly defining the expected functionalities for each user role: Student Learner, Student Tutor, Teacher Moderator, and Administrator. This list will serve as the foundation for the Product Backlog and will guide the development of all six core modules of Katuwang.

**3.2.2 User Design**  
After establishing system requirements, the study will proceed to the User Design phase, which focuses on creating prototypes and visual representations of Katuwang's interface. This phase aligns with Agile principles that promote iterative development and continuous stakeholder feedback through working prototypes (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020), and will function as an essential bridge between requirements planning and actual system development. During this phase, the research team will design wireframes and high-fidelity UI/UX prototypes for all major system components, including the student tutoring request and tutor profile browsing pages, the tutor session management and progress tracking interfaces, the chatbot assistant module, and the administrator analytics dashboard.  These prototypes will be presented to the principal and selected faculty of TRIS for evaluation, focusing on usability, navigation flow, visual clarity, and alignment with the school's operational needs. Feedback gathered during this stage will guide design refinements before implementation begins.  The finalized design will serve as the official blueprint for development, ensuring that the system is intentionally shaped around the needs of TRIS students rather than being improvised during coding.

### **3.2.3 Product Backlog**

Following the design phase, all identified system features and requirements will be organized into a structured Product Backlog. Each Product Backlog Item (PBI) will include a brief description of the task, specify the team members to whom it is assigned, and indicate its priority and estimated timeline (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). The backlog for Katuwang will be arranged according to system importance, with core and high-impact features such as the tutor-learner matching algorithm, the session management module, the user authentication and role-based access control system, and the pre- and post-test assessment module, positioned at the top of the backlog and addressed first during sprint execution. Each PBI will be directly linked to specific system objectives, maintaining alignment between the study's goals and implementation tasks. As development progresses and feedback is gathered from TRIS stakeholders, the backlog will be continuously updated to reflect changing priorities and newly identified requirements, ensuring that the most critical features are consistently developed ahead of lower-priority enhancements.

### **3.2.4 Sprint Planning**

With the product backlog established, sprint planning sessions will be conducted to determine which tasks will be completed in each development cycle. Agile project planning organizes work into iterative units called sprints, wherein the Scrum Master identifies the product backlog items for the upcoming cycle, logs them on the scrum board, and sets the sprint duration based on resource availability and team capacity (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). In this study, the research team leader will assume the role of the Scrum Master, responsible for organizing sprint planning sessions, distributing tasks among team members, and maintaining the scrum board. Each sprint will focus on a specific set of related modules or features, and its duration will be determined by task complexity and the team's available working hours within their academic schedule.

### **3.2.5 Daily Scrum**

During each sprint, the team will hold regular Daily Scrum meetings to monitor progress and address any challenges encountered. These short, structured meetings promote transparency, coordination, and the timely resolution of issues by reviewing ongoing tasks and identifying obstacles (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). The Scrum Master will oversee task progress using a digital scrum board, which visually tracks the status of all development activities and ensures that tasks remain aligned with the sprint timeline. Any technical or implementation issues encountered during the development of Katuwang's modules such as difficulties in implementing the weighted scoring algorithm for tutor-learner matching or configuring the intent-based chatbot logic, will be discussed during these sessions and resolved promptly. This continuous communication promotes accountability, minimizes delays, and ensures that all team members remain coordinated throughout each sprint.

### 

### 

### **3.2.6 Sprint Release**

At the end of each sprint, a Sprint Release will be conducted, wherein the completed system features are presented to the principal and selected faculty of TRIS for review. This allows stakeholders to interact with a working increment of the platform and evaluate its effectiveness in addressing the school's academic support needs. Feedback gathered during this stage will be documented and used to guide subsequent improvements. This regular delivery of functional system increments to the end-users is a core principle of Agile, as it enables continuous stakeholder involvement and ensures that development remains aligned with the actual needs of TRIS students and faculty rather than assumptions made at the outset of the project (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020).

### **3.2.7 Retrospective**

After each sprint release, the team will conduct a Retrospective meeting to evaluate the completed sprint before proceeding to the next development cycle. This session provides an opportunity to reflect on what was accomplished, identify challenges encountered during development, and determine areas for process improvement (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). The team will discuss what worked well, what issues arose, and how workflows can be refined in subsequent sprints. Feedback from TRIS stakeholders gathered during the sprint release will also be reviewed during this session to identify necessary adjustments or enhancements to the platform. Insights gained from the retrospective will directly inform the planning and execution of the following sprint, supporting continuous improvement throughout the project lifecycle.

### **3.2.8 Refinement**

The final recurring phase in each sprint cycle is Refinement, during which the Product Backlog will be reviewed and updated based on the outcomes of the recently completed sprint and feedback from stakeholders. This process ensures that all remaining tasks remain relevant, clearly defined, and accurately estimated (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). During refinement sessions, the team will revisit backlog items scheduled for upcoming sprints to incorporate any new requirements or modifications surfaced during the sprint release and retrospective. Any requested changes from the principal and selected faculty of TRIS such as additions to the chatbot knowledge base, adjustments to the tutor assessment criteria, or modifications to the analytics dashboard reports, will be formally documented and integrated into the backlog during this phase. This continuous updating process keeps the backlog accurate and ensures that the development plan remains aligned with TRIS's evolving needs and the study's objectives across all remaining development cycles.

# **3.3 Evaluation procedure**

The system will be evaluated based on the ISO/IEC 25010\. This evaluation mechanism is designed to assess how effectively the Katuwang platform addresses academic support gaps at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School (TRIS). A structured survey questionnaire will be distributed to the four primary user groups, Student Learners, Student Tutors, Teacher Moderators, and Administrators, following a supervised hands-on orientation and walkthrough of the platform. This ensures that all respondents provide feedback grounded in direct experience with the system's core features, such as tutor matching and the chatbot assistant. The evaluation results will be statistically analyzed using a 5-point Likert scale to determine the overall effectiveness and quality of the platform as shown in Table 4\. The Weighted Mean (WM) will be computed for each quality dimension and interpreted using the following scale:

***Table 7\.** Likert Scale Evaluation*

| Range | Rating | Description |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 5 | Strongly Agree | The system demonstrates very high compliance with Functional Suitability, Performance Efficiency, Interaction Capability, Reliability, Flexibility, and Security.  |
| 4 | Agree | The system demonstrates high compliance with the evaluated ISO/IEC 25010 attributes.  |
| 3 | Neutral | The system demonstrates moderate compliance with the evaluated attributes.  |
| 2 | Disagree | The system demonstrates low compliance with the evaluated attributes.  |
| 1 | Strongly Disagree  | The system demonstrates very low or no compliance with the evaluated attributes.  |

# **CHAPTER II**

# **THEORETICAL FRAMEWORK**

## **2.1 Review of Related Literature**	 	The theoretical and conceptual underpinnings of Katuwang, a web-based peer tutoring management system for secondary students at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School are described in this chapter.  The review examines current research on academic skill gaps, peer tutoring effectiveness, out-of-field teaching, benefits of technology-mediated environments on program management, and data privacy in educational technology systems by drawing on both domestic and foreign literature.

## **2.1.1 Academic Skill Gap**

	Academic skill gaps are the differences between students' actual demonstrated proficiency and the competencies they should have mastered at a particular grade level.  These gaps are especially significant in reading and math, two fundamental learning domains since deficiencies in these areas compound across grade levels and eventually students' ability to interact with higher-order academic content.  To support the need for focused additional academic interventions like Katuwang it is crucial to comprehend the nature causes and persistence of academic skill gaps. 

Scammacca et al. (2020) used cohort-sequential latent growth curve models over a two-year period to analyze reading and math growth patterns among 5900 students in Grades 1 through 5\. According to their findings there are still achievement gaps between students from higher-income and lower-income families and understanding how growth varies depending on initial proficiency grade and demographics is essential to reducing these gaps.  According to the study, there is a cumulative disadvantage pattern whereby students who perform worse over time tend to lag behind rather than catch up.  This directly relates to Katuwang because it demonstrates how important it is for students in low-income areas like those in Taysan Resettlement to receive early and ongoing additional academic support. 

In their analysis of the COVID-19 pandemic’s effects on mathematics education Sooknanan and Seemungal (2023) contended that school disruptions caused by the pandemic constituted a public health emergency that was further mishandled by misinterpreting educational data.  According to their study, students' learning of mathematics significantly decreased as a result of school closures because they needed more remediation before moving on to new lessons which widened already-existing learning gaps across grade levels.  This is pertinent to Katuwang because many of the senior high school students at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School went through their early middle school years during the disruptions of the pandemic which directly led to their current skill gaps. 

In order to investigate generational trends in foundational learning among students in Grades 4–10, Igarashi and Suryadarma (2023) examined three rounds of nationally representative household survey data from the Philippines from 2003 to 2019\. According to their analysis not all Filipino students in higher grades have mastered the fundamental reading and math skills taught in Grade 3, and the decline in foundational math skills between the 2003 and 2019 cohorts suggests that the Philippine basic education system has significant structural limitations.  Crucially, this long-term result predates the pandemic demonstrating that academic skill gaps in the Philippines are a long-standing systemic failure rather than a recent occurrence.  This study is important because it shows that the reading and math challenges that senior high school students at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School face are a part of a larger well-established national trend. 

Aguhayon et al. (2023) tested whether differentiated instruction could close math learning gaps in a single school term by conducting an action research study in a Philippine secondary school.  Their findings demonstrated that differentiated instruction was effective in filling in students' learning gaps even in a brief amount of time and that it also boosted students' confidence in their ability to solve math problems.  Although encouraging, the study notes that teacher availability and capacity are critical for consistent implementation, a limitation that is particularly noticeable in public schools with limited funding.  This result supports the idea that student-driven supplemental methods like Katuwang can be used in addition to traditional classroom instruction. 

Baiño (2025) synthesized results from national assessments and classroom-level interventions to examine post-pandemic challenges in addressing student learning gaps in Philippine public schools. Only one out of five Filipino participants met the minimum proficiency level in mathematics literacy according to the study and learning gaps were more noticeable among students attending public schools. Notably Baiño also noted that students who received peer tutoring demonstrated better learning outcomes than those in traditional lecture settings indicating that peer tutoring was one of the best tactics teachers used to overcome learning loss. This study is particularly pertinent because it simultaneously shows how serious the skill gap issue is and confirms that Katuwang's main tactic peer tutoring is an effective solution in the context of Philippine public schools. 

The reviewed studies collectively demonstrate the persistence, cumulative nature, and socioeconomic pattern of academic skill gaps in mathematics and reading. Global investigation by Scammacca et al. (2020) and Sooknanan and Seemungal (2023) show that these gaps grow over time and are exacerbated by systemic disruptions like the COVID-19 pandemic, while research conducted in the Philippines by Igarashi and Suryadarma (2023) and Baiño (2025) reveals the severity of foundational deficiencies that are worsening at the secondary level are both generational. Additionally the effectiveness of focused interventions as documented by Aguhayon et al. (2023) and the efficacy of peer tutoring emphasized by Baiño (2025) together confirm that organized student-driven assistance is a workable remedy for this learning crisis. The creation of Katuwang, a web-based peer tutoring platform intended to offer easily accessible and organized academic support to students at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School who still struggle despite years of formal education, was justified by these findings. 

## **2.1.2 Out of Field Teaching**

Teachers' content mastery is crucial to education but systemic teacher shortages frequently force schools to assign teachers to subjects outside of their formal specialization. Particularly, in foundational and STEM subjects, this phenomenon known as out-of-field teaching creates substantial instructional gaps. Comprehending this difficulty is extremely pertinent to this research since it demonstrates why traditional classroom instruction is occasionally inadequate for student mastery. It supports the need for additional student-led interventions to guarantee that students develop the fundamental skills needed for postsecondary education. 

Luft et al. (2020) investigated the systemic difficulties associated with teaching science outside of one's field. As these educators frequently lack the specialized pedagogical content knowledge needed to explain complex scientific concepts, their study found that assigning teachers outside of their formal qualifications significantly compromises instructional quality.  This is relevant to Katuwang as it establishes the root cause of the foundational learning gaps that students face in core subjects.

The wider ramifications of out-of-field instruction in various educational systems were examined by Hobbs and Porsch (2021). They discovered that this practice not only has a detrimental impact on students' learning outcomes but also puts teachers under a great deal of stress which lowers the standard of instruction. Katuwang highlights the need for outside academic support systems to keep students from falling behind due to the instructional deficiencies brought on by out-of-field assignments. 

Goos et al. (2020) created and assessed a nationwide blended learning initiative to give out-of-field math teachers cooperative professional development. According to their research, digital platforms that encourage peer collaboration are very successful in helping educators fill in knowledge gaps.  This validates the suggested system by demonstrating that collaborative digital and structured environments can effectively address systemic academic deficiencies. 

Bajar et al. (2021) investigated the School Learning Action Cell (SLAC) as an out-of-field teaching intervention in a rural Southern Philippine school. According to their research SLAC is an essential cooperative solution where teachers exchange localized practices and expertise to close the instructional gaps brought on by assignments that are outside of their field of expertise. This is extremely pertinent because it demonstrates how the Philippine basic education system, which mirrors the fundamental idea of the suggested student tutoring platform, primarily depends on structured peer-to-peer collaborative frameworks to overcome instructional deficits.

Bocar et al. (2023) looked more closely at how out-of-field instruction affected the motivation and self-efficacy of teachers in nearby Philippine schools. According to their findings teaching confidence had significantly declined which frequently led to lessons being taught at a superficial level in order to avoid the shame of non-mastery.  Students need alternative ways to fully comprehend subjects that are only briefly covered in their formal classes which directly supports the need for the proposed study. 

All of the reviewed studies demonstrate how out-of-field instruction seriously impedes the delivery of foundational education resulting in instructional gaps that call for digital and collaborative interventions. Luft et al. (2020) as well as Bocar et al. (2023)  studies shows how a lack of subject specialization undermines teaching effectiveness and self-efficacy while Hobbs and Porsch (2021) discover that this practice has a detrimental impact on student learning outcomes and puts teachers under a great deal of stress which lowers the quality of education delivery. Moreover research conducted by Goos et al. (2020) as well as Bajar et al. (2023) underline that peer-led frameworks like the School Learning Action Cell (SLAC) and digital platforms are essential solutions for the Philippines systemic shortcomings. According to recent data from the International Journal of Instruction (2026), out-of-field teaching continues to be a significant obstacle to national STEM proficiency necessitating prompt localized assistance. When taken as a whole these results provide strong support for Katuwang, which fills in these localized instructional gaps at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School by enlisting skilled students to offer the academic scaffolding and outside support systems needed for students to succeed in spite of structural classroom constraints.

##  **2.1.3 Peer tutoring and its effectiveness**

Peer tutoring has long been acknowledged as a cost-effective evidence-based intervention that can greatly enhance student outcomes. Peer-led instruction enables students to engage in a less formal, more relaxed setting than a traditional classroom by promoting a culture of shared responsibility and cooperation. The learning by teaching effect is covered in this section. Both the tutor and the student gain from strengthened knowledge, boosted self-esteem, and the development of crucial leadership and communication skills. 

Based on 24 studies with 3311 participants, Hidayat and Saad (2025) carried out a thorough meta-analysis of the effects of peer tutoring in STEM fields which was published in the International Journal of Educational Research Open. Regardless of whether same-age or cross-age peer tutoring is used the study clearly shows a significant impact on STEM subject achievement with a high ES of 1\. 23 demonstrating the effectiveness of peer tutoring in supporting academic achievement. According to the study peer tutoring can be successful in both in-person and online settings which gives digital solutions like Katuwang a strong foundation. According to the study's findings peer tutoring is an efficient and cooperative teaching strategy that produces better results in a variety of contexts supporting the foundation of a platform such as Katuwang. 

Al Yahyaei et al. (2024) studied how peer tutoring affected first-year nursing students at Sultan Qaboos University's academic performance, psychological empowerment and student satisfaction; the results were published in SAGE Open Nursing. The authors discovered that the peer tutoring approach greatly enhanced the students academic achievement. Additionally the authors discovered that the students psychological empowerment was enhanced by the peer tutoring approach because they felt empowered and confident in their ability to learn and seek assistance. Because the peer tutoring approach is multifaceted and has a positive impact on students academic performance and psychological empowerment the authors suggested that it be used in professional course settings. Katuwang's primary objective is to use a single platform to make the peer tutoring approach available.

Alegre et al. (2019) used a quasi-experimental pretest-posttest design to investigate the effects of peer tutoring on mathematics achievement among 89 students from Grades 1 4 7 and 9 in primary and secondary education under comparable settings. Both primary and secondary education saw notable improvements according to their statistical analysis and there were no appreciable variations in the increases in students' grades between educational levels. This result disproved previous research that suggested younger students gain more from peer tutoring and demonstrated that peer tutoring in mathematics consistently improves academic performance across grade levels. This is especially pertinent to the current study which emphasizes that peer tutoring is still a successful intervention even at the secondary level.

According to research published in the International Journal of Social Science and Education Research Studies, Lagmay and Bacena (2025) investigated the impact of peer tutoring on the mathematical performance of Filipino students in Grade 10\. The study found that when peer tutoring techniques were used students' attitudes and performance in mathematics improved. The authors used PISA’s 2022 results which showed that the Philippines ranked 76th out of 81 countries with an average score of 355 for mathematical literacy while the OECD as a whole scored 489 to set the scene for this problem. Peer tutoring according to the authors is a potential remedy for this enduring issue and this study demonstrates the necessity and applicability of Katuwang as a system that can aid in closing the achievement gap among Filipino students. 

Students in Grade 2 at Gabi Central Elementary School in Davao de Oro participated in a study by Atamosa and Dioso (2024) on the effectiveness of structured peer instruction. It was discovered prior to the study that many students were performing at levels below accepted English benchmarks. However it was noted that the students academic performance significantly improved after they took part in structured peer tutoring reaching a Very Satisfactory level that was almost mastery. Statistical analysis also revealed a direct correlation between this improvement and peer tutoring interventions which greatly increased students involvement and engagement in academic work. The study's use of peer tutoring as a successful strategy for filling academic gaps in rural areas where extra academic support at home might not be sufficient makes it pertinent to Katuwang. It is also pertinent to Katuwang's objective of establishing a setting in which students can actively participate in academic work and support one another in achieving academic success. Furthermore it lends credence to the idea that peer tutoring can be successful even in situations where students' proficiency falls short of basic requirements. 

Anonuevo (2024) examined the impact of the ParadeigMath peer tutoring program on the attitudes and math performance of Baras Rural Development High School students in Grade 9\. The primary objective of the study which took place during the 2023–2024 academic year was to assist students in strengthening their algebraic abilities which they had neglected during the pandemic. According to the study, students' mean score improved from 18 point 31 on the pre-test to 29 point 62 on the post-test. Qualitative findings on the other hand showed that the tutors had a sense of pride, service and responsibility and that the tutees found learning to be simple because they were guided through a methodical process using real-world examples. This study is pertinent to our research Katuwang since it demonstrates that peer tutoring programs can be a successful way to raise academic achievement in the Philippines. 

The long-running Tulong-Dunong (TD) tutoring program at Ateneo de Manila was examined by Adan (2022) who concentrated on its adaptability and resilience during the COVID-19 pandemic. The literature emphasizes the importance of peer tutoring as a platform for socio-emotional connection pointing out that students had a thirst for human interaction and the chance to assist others when face-to-face activities were suspended. The program showed that peer-to-peer relationships can overcome physical barriers when they are supported by deliberate coordination and planning by shifting to an online environment through video tutorials and virtual sessions. For Katuwang this reflection is extremely important because it supports the notion that a tutoring platform is not just a means of academic transfer in subjects like math and English but also an essential setting for developing empathy, a sense of community and love in deeds in the Philippine educational setting. Furthermore the program’s emphasis on kwentuhan (dialogue) to understand a learner's social context reinforces Katuwang’s objective of creating a peer-led environment that is approachable, culturally relevant and sensitive to the holistic needs of the student. 

According to Hartenstein (2024) the use of buddy systems in schools is a complex peer-mediated approach that includes inclusive education, social support and academic tutoring. The study highlights that these systems are two-way benefiting both the tutee and the tutor by fostering leadership empathy and communication skills. This more comprehensive view of peer-to-peer support is consistent with Katuwang's volunteer-based structure implying that the platform can function as a digital infrastructure for a school-wide buddy system that promotes inclusive social settings and academic success. Buddy system that fosters both academic achievement and inclusive social environments. 

The effectiveness of near-peer mentoring in building student resilience is examined by Paw (2025) especially in light of the social and communication skill gaps made worse by isolation brought on by the pandemic. The project demonstrates that although academic tutoring plays a major role, the intervention is essentially a socio-emotional one that fosters human flourishing for both the mentor and the mentee as well as empathy and mutual support. These results offer a theoretical basis for Katuwang's goal of using peer interaction to help students overcome both academic obstacles and the more general emotional difficulties they encounter in today's educational environment. 

A study that was published in the International Journal of Arts Sciences and Education (2022) looked at the Buddy System as a way to improve the reading comprehension of Filipino students in Grade 5\. The results highlight the effectiveness of structured peer-led strategies in resolving literacy issues and enhancing student comprehension. This body of research backs up Katuwang's goal of offering an organized digital setting where peer-to-peer assistance can be used to close particular learning gaps in a variety of subject areas and grade levels.  

HELM Learning, a peer-to-peer learning platform created to support collaborative education where students can both teach and learn in an organized online setting was introduced by Anantha (2022). By encouraging students to participate in instructional activities the platform fosters active learning and improves comprehension, collaboration and communication abilities. In addition to peer interaction, HELM incorporates automated systems like communication tools, user management and class scheduling to enhance the overall learning process and platform effectiveness. Additionally it includes a machine learning-based recommendation system that supports individualized learning pathways by recommending pertinent courses based on students' prior learning habits and interests. The study emphasizes how automation and intelligent systems can be used to improve accessibility engagement and learning continuity on peer-to-peer learning platforms. These results are pertinent to the current study Katuwang because they highlight the wider benefits of AI-supported peer learning environments in enhancing student engagement, collaboration and resource accessibility, not just through matching systems but also through overall learning experience enhancement. 

Peer tutoring is a multifaceted high-impact intervention that successfully meets students academic and socio-emotional needs in a variety of settings according to the reviewed literature. Global research by Alegre et al. and Hidayat and Saad (2025). Al Yahyaei et al. (2020). (2024) as well as studies conducted in the Philippines by Lagmay and Bacena (2025) Atamosa and Dioso (2024) and Añonuevo (2024) all confirm that structured peer-led academic support in reading and math is a very successful method for closing proficiency gaps. Additionally the technological frameworks suggested by Anantha (2022) and the qualitative findings from Adan (2022) Hartenstein (2024) and Paw (2025) show that digital peer-learning environments promote crucial socio-emotional resilience and empathy. These results give Katuwang that it is a platform specifically created to operationalize structured voluntary and technology-mediated academic support to address the learning crisis among students at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School which is a strong and solid rationale. 

## **2.1.4 Advantage of a technology-mediated environment for managing tutoring programs**

Even though conventional peer tutoring works well, incorporating technology offers a scalable and structured framework that manual systems frequently lack. Centralized management, effective session scheduling and the development of on-demand digital resource repositories are all made possible by a web-based environment like Katuwang. This review demonstrates how digital platforms provide administrators with real-time data to monitor academic support and guarantee the security and effectiveness of peer interactions thereby overcoming the logistical challenges associated with organizing student volunteers.  

As reported in the Asian Journal Collaboration of Social Environmental and Education, Libastari and Ziqri (2026) created a web-based Knowledge Center named U-NeedKC to address the low level of digital writing and knowledge-sharing culture among higher education students. In order to meet the requirements of Industry 5\. 0 the study concentrated on creating a system that facilitates cooperation training and adaptable access to academic information. The results showed that a lack of an integrated knowledge-sharing environment, low digital literacy underutilization of current LMS features and technical limitations were among the difficulties that educators and students had to deal with. In response, the study suggested a platform that combines workshops, discussion boards, digital archives course modules and a point-based system to promote participation. The system is anticipated to enhance learning participation information accessibility collaboration and digital competencies despite the fact that it has not yet undergone comprehensive testing. These results are pertinent to the current study Katuwang since they emphasize how crucial it is to include collaborative features and user-centered design in order to encourage student participation and knowledge sharing. 

Cha et al. (2025) investigated how networking agency and technology self-efficacy affected elementary students' participation in technology-mediated learning settings. The study which was published in the British Journal of Educational Technology concentrated on fourth and fifth grade students who received both online and in-person instruction. The researchers discovered that networking agencies significantly predicted engagement in fully online learning contexts whereas technology self-efficacy had a greater impact in face-to-face settings using hierarchical regression and moderation analyses. In task-based group activities the networking agency also served as a moderator especially when students felt less at ease working together virtually. According to the findings, maintaining student engagement in digital learning environments requires both technical confidence and collaborative communication skills. These findings are pertinent to the current study Katuwang since they highlight how crucial it is to incorporate collaborative interaction and technical usability features into educational platforms in order to improve learner engagement and participation. 

For a peer tutoring program at Nanyang Technological University, Shan and Xinyi (2024) created a web-based application. This was motivated by the need to replace manual traditional methods of managing peer tutoring programs with a centralized digital platform that enables smooth communication, effective class scheduling and thorough progress tracking for both student tutors and learners. According to the study, formalizing peer tutoring coordination through a web-based system greatly lessens the administrative load while improving academic support organization accessibility and transparency for all parties. This directly relates to Katuwang because both systems function as platforms for coordination between student tutors and learners replacing informal or paper-based arrangements with a structured digital environment rather than as instructional tools in and of themselves. Shan's system was created in a well-resourced university setting but Katuwang expands it into a more underprivileged setting, a public integrated school that serves a resettlement community by adding an analytics dashboard that is specifically designed to meet the administrative requirements of primary and secondary education in the Philippine public school system a weighted scoring tutor-learner matching algorithm and a navigation support chatbot. 

Kayanja et al. (2025) examined the systemic transition to fully automated and paperless platforms as part of the ongoing digital transformation in educational settings. According to their findings moving from manual traditional workflows to centralized digital systems greatly increases organizational efficiency by streamlining administrative procedures and improving data accessibility. The goal of digitizing the manual conventional peer-tutoring model at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School is theoretically justified by this study which makes it extremely pertinent to the suggested platform. In addition to lowering logistical challenges for student volunteers the system ensures a more effective long-lasting and easily monitored academic support framework by moving session scheduling resource sharing and post-session observation logging into a centralized paperless online environment. 

According to Garet and Zeiser (2023) technology-assisted tutoring programs speed up learning by offering a scalable framework that is frequently absent from manual systems. According to their report, research-based tutoring can reach a large number of students through digital platforms especially in high-poverty districts with limited in-person resources. According to the study, data-driven customization is made possible by technology-mediated environments which use ongoing assessment to produce recommendations in real time that tailor instruction to each student. Additionally it has been demonstrated that integrating virtual workspaces with interactive tools and multi-media scaffolding enhances the learning process and helps tutors better monitor students progress through automated dashboards. Katuwang's goal of utilizing a web-based environment to overcome logistical obstacles and offer organized academic support is supported by this body of literature. 

## **2.1.5 Data Privacy in Educational Technology Systems** 

A strong framework for data privacy is required when integrating digital platforms into schools particularly when managing the records of minors. Republic Act No. 10173 or The Data Privacy Act of 2012 is the Philippine law that mandates the protection of sensitive personal data in both the public and private spheres. In the context of educational technology this calls for Privacy by Design which guarantees that data protection is incorporated into the system from the outset. 

In the framework of the Data Privacy Act of 2012 (RA 10173), Anicete and Ambat (2024) investigated the degree of data privacy awareness and compliance among Philippine educational stakeholders. According to their research, even though digital transformation is happening quickly, parents and students in underprivileged communities still lack privacy literacy which frequently results in the unintentional disclosure of private information. In order to guarantee that the students and families of Taysan Resettlement are legally protected and fully aware of their digital rights, it is imperative to incorporate robust data protection features and simplified privacy notices.

Garcia et al. (2025) focused on the idea of Data Minimization in student-led platforms while examining the ethical ramifications of data-driven educational tools. The results highlighted that in order to lower the risk of identity theft or social profiling educational systems should only gather the data points that are absolutely necessary for their particular function. This is very important for Katuwang because it supports the systems design which excludes superfluous personal identifiers that might jeopardize the security of students in favor of storing only pertinent academic data such as grade levels and particular subject difficulties. 

In a recent assessment of cybersecurity trends in Philippine basic education, Veloso and Martinez (2026) focused on the susceptibility of school-based web applications to unauthorized data breaches. They maintained that Privacy by Design in which security measures are integrated into the source code rather than added as an afterthought is necessary for technology-mediated interventions to be sustainable. This result demonstrates that safeguarding student data is just as essential to academic success as the tutoring material itself supporting Katuwang's goal of offering a safe online environment. 

All of the reviewed literature demonstrates that data privacy is an essential component of contemporary educational technology particularly when it comes to serving underprivileged minority populations. Studies by Garcia et al. and Anicete and Ambat (2024). (2025) emphasizes the need to minimize data and obtain informed consent in order to protect sensitive student information both legally and ethically. Additionally Veloso and Martinez (2026) offered the technical rationale for putting in place secure role-based access and privacy-first architectures. All of these studies confirm that Katuwang must function in a safe technologically mediated setting that respects each student's privacy and dignity in order to successfully close academic skill gaps at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School.

## **2.2 Gap Bridged by the study**

The reviewed literature continually addresses instructional inefficiencies as a trouble caused by systemic disasters and useful resource obstacles, but none especially examines a centralized, volunteer-driven virtual platform tailor-made for the particular socio-economic context of Philippine resettlement faculties. Most existing research (e.g., Scammacca et al., 2020; Sooknanan and Seemungal, 2023\) focus on documenting the severity of educational talent gaps and pandemic-precipitated getting to know loss, without providing localized, scalable infrastructure for intervention. Even as conventional mentorship models just like the Tulong-Dunong program foster important human connections, they continually lack a scalable, centralized virtual infrastructure required to coordinate volunteers and distribute resources effectively. Studies on technology-mediated environments further reinforce this gap. Libastari and Ziqri (2026) and Cha et al. (2025) established that the absence of integrated collaborative features and technical usability remain persistent barriers in educational platforms, while Shan and Xinyi (2024) demonstrated the viability of web-based peer tutoring coordination only within a well-resourced university setting. Kayanja et al. (2025) and Garet and Zeiser (2023) confirmed the efficiency gains of digital transition and technology-assisted tutoring respectively, yet neither addresses the specific operational needs of Philippine public schools under limited resources and out-of-field teaching conditions. Katuwang fills these gaps by systematizing informal "peer assist" into a centralized, low-barrier internet platform that, without delay, mitigates academic deficits, which includes those caused by out-of-field teaching and the COVID-19 pandemic. Not like generalized instructional systems, it operates strictly on a volunteerism model driven via the cultural fee of Bayanihan, getting rid of the economic obstacles usually associated with personal tutoring for Grade 7-12 students. Katuwang introduces a specialized position structure regarding student tutors, learners, and instructor moderators, incorporating a weighted matching algorithm and an intent-based chatbot tailor-made to the particular operational and administrative requirements of Taysan Resettlement incorporated school. By operationalizing the mandates of Republic Act No. 12028 ARAL Program Act at the local level, this study provides a sustainable, student-driven remedy to generational educational disparities that existing literature has not yet fully addressed. 

## **2.3 Concept of the Study**

An Input-Process-Output (IPO) model serves as a representation of the conceptual framework of this study, showing how academic needs, skills, and system interactions flow within the Katuwang platform and are converted into structured tutoring support. By identifying the data it receives, the development and integration processes it undergoes, and the final platform that facilitates user collaboration, the IPO model acts as a guide for understanding how the system functions. 

| Input | Process | Output |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Student learners Student tutor volunteers Teachers | Development of the *Katuwang* system including design, implementation, and integration of features for user interaction, management, and coordination | A centralized and organized platform for academic support and collaboration among students, tutors, and teachers |

***Figure 1\.** Input-Process-Output (IPO) Model of Katuwang*

As shown in Figure 1, the input component involves the students abilities and academic requirements that make up the system's input component. This covers both the learning needs, challenges and subject requirements of student learners as well as the subject strengths and areas of expertise of student tutors. These inputs provide the main information needed to comprehend how students can help each other in the tutoring setting. 

The process component involves the creation of the Katuwang system including its design implementation and integration of its essential features. The development of a useful platform that can effectively handle user interaction coordination and academic support is the main goal of this phase. In order to provide efficient tutoring services it guarantees that the system appropriately manages data pertaining to student abilities and requirements. 

The output component is a centralized tutoring management platform that links instructors, student tutors and students in an orderly system. It offers an organized setting for academic assistance facilitating effective teamwork, communication, and tutoring service access on a single platform. 

The feedback component of the system gathers and analyzes user-generated data following tutoring sessions with the goal of continuously improving the Katuwang platform. This includes comments from students on the efficacy of tutors, the caliber of sessions and learning objectives as well as observations and assessments from teachers keeping an eye on the development of students in class groups. 

## **2.4 Definition of Terms**

The following terms are defined according to their specific application within the context of this study and are arranged in alphabetical order:

**ARAL Program Act (Republic Act No. 12028\)** – The national legislation geared toward addressing getting to know loss through established intervention applications, which Katuwang seeks to operationalize at the nearby level. 

**Bayanihan** – The voluntary and community-pushed nature of the Katuwang platform, wherein pupil tutors freely provide their time and understanding to assist fellow students without monetary compensation, reflecting a shared feel of educational responsibility inside the faculty community. 

**Data Minimization** – The principle of accumulating only the strictly necessary statistics factors required for gadget functionality—together with grade degrees and concern problems—to reduce privateness risks for minors. 

**Foundational Skills** – The analyzing comprehension and mathematical proficiency talents that primary and secondary students at Taysan Resettlement incorporated faculty are expected to expand and that the Katuwang platform pursuits to reinforce through established peer tutoring sessions. 

**ISO/IEC 25010** – The software program exceptionally well known used to evaluate the Katuwang platform specifically in phrases of purposeful suitability, performance efficiency, usability, reliability, and safety during the trying out phase. 

**Katuwang** – a web-based totally peer tutoring control platform designed as a voluntary, no-charge educational aid gadget for Taysan Resettlement incorporated faculty, connecting pupil tutors with novices thru flexible peer getting to know sessions. 

**Learner** – a primary or secondary pupil enrolled at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School who registers at the Katuwang platform to request and acquire educational help from a pupil tutor in a single or greater concern region. 

**Learning Analytics** – The module inside Katuwang that procedures session statistics, participation logs, and feedback to generate actionable insights, together with identifying at-danger novices and tutor performance developments. 

**Out-of-Field Teaching** – the educational situation at Taysan Resettlement incorporated faculty wherein instructors are assigned to topics outside their region of specialization, growing foundational getting to know gaps that the platform pursuits to cope with. 

**Peer Learning** – The flexible instructional model followed by means of Katuwang, in which a single pupil tutor allows educational sessions with one or greater novices concurrently to grow the reach and efficiency of educational help. 

**Peer Tutoring** – The established educational aid sessions facilitated thru the Katuwang platform, wherein registered pupil tutors offer steering to novices in particular topics, managed through scheduling and tracking capabilities. 

**Privacy by Design** – an improvement technique wherein safety protocols and statistics protection capabilities are incorporated without delay into the source code from the beginning of improvement.

**Privacy Literacy** – the extent of consciousness amongst students and parents regarding the protection of sensitive private records and their virtual rights. 

**Session** – a scheduled peer tutoring assembly facilitated through the Katuwang platform, managed through scheduling and tracking modules, wherein a registered tutor provides educational help to one or greater novices. 

**Teacher Moderator** – a faculty body of workers at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School authorized to monitor session sports, assessment analytics, and ensure tutoring interactions align with educational goals. 

**Tutor** – a man or woman, inclusive of students enrolled at Taysan Resettlement incorporated faculty or outside volunteers, who registers at the Katuwang platform to voluntarily provide educational help in topics wherein they demonstrate competence and hobby. 

**Usability** – The measure of the way without problems and correctly goal users, inclusive of students and teacher moderators, can navigate the platform to accomplish obligations together with registration, scheduling, and tutor similar. 

**Voluntary Participation** – The non-compulsory and unpaid nature of pupil tutor involvement in Katuwang, pushed by means of intrinsic motivation and a feel of community responsibility in place of monetary incentive. internet-based totally 

**Web-Based Platform** – the nature of Katuwang as a browser-handy system hosted on the internet, designed to be usable on any device with connectivity without requiring software program set up.

 

 

# **📙 Chapter III**

# **CHAPTER III**

# **MATERIALS AND METHODS**

This chapter presents the strategies, tools, and methods utilized in the development of Katuwang. It outlines the materials, the software engineering framework, and the procedures used to evaluate the system’s functionality, performance, and user acceptability. 

# **3.1 Materials**

The development and deployment of the Katuwang platform will require specific technical environments for both the research team and the end-users. For the development team, the system will be built using laptops running Windows 11 and Debian 13 Linux Based operating system, which will serve as the primary workstations for coding, testing, and debugging the platform. For the end-users, the platform will be accessible through any desktop or mobile device equipped with a modern web browser and a stable internet connection, requiring no additional software installation, consistent with the web-based nature of the system. 

## **3.1.1 Software** 

The development of Katuwang, a web-based management platform will utilize a modern full-stack technology stack to ensure a responsive, accessible, and secure web-based peer tutoring environment for students, teachers, and administrators.  The specific software versions identified in the preceding section are shown in Table 1\.

For system development, Next.js 16.2.4 and React 19.2 will be used to build a responsive and mobile-adaptive user interface that supports various screen sizes and user roles. Tailwind CSS 4.0 will be applied for efficient, utility-first styling to maintain consistency across the platform. TypeScript will also be used alongside React and Next.js to improve code reliability, maintainability, and type safety during development.

Server-side logic will be handled through Next.js API Routes, which will manage core system processes such as user management, session scheduling, and data processing. Node.js will serve as the runtime environment that enables JavaScript execution on the server side.

For database management, Prisma 7.7.0 will be used as the Object-Relational Mapping (ORM) tool that will act as an interface between the application and MySQL 9.7.0, allowing efficient, type-safe, and structured database operations.

MySQL 9.7.0 will be utilized as the relational database management system to securely store and organize structured data, including user profiles, tutoring session records, and system-related information.

For authentication and user session management, NextAuth.js 4.24.14 will be implemented to provide secure login and role-based access control for different users of the platform.

For version control and collaborative development, Git will be used to track changes in the source code, while GitHub will serve as the remote repository hosting platform, enabling team collaboration, code backup, and version management throughout the development process.

Google Chrome will be used as the primary browser for development, testing, and performance profiling due to its built-in developer tools. Additionally, Visual Studio Code will be used as the main integrated development environment (IDE) for coding, debugging, and project management, while Figma will be utilized during the design phase to create high-fidelity wireframes and user interface prototypes for the different user roles prior to system implementation.
