## **3.1.3 System Security and Algorithmic Design**

	The creation of Katuwang, a web-based platform that serves secondary students including minors, requires thoughtful and well-founded choices regarding system security and fundamental algorithmic design. In addition to choosing a technology stack, the research team assessed particular implementation decisions that have an immediate impact on the platform's efficacy, safety and integrity. This section explains the reasoning behind three crucial design choices: using a weighted scoring algorithm to match student learners with the best peer tutor, adopting Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) as the access management model to enforce user permissions, and choosing bcrypt as the password hashing algorithm to secure user credentials. In order to ensure that the selected strategies are not only technically sound but also proportionate to the operational scale resource limitations and data privacy obligations of a school-based platform serving the Taysan Resettlement Integrated School community, each decision was made by comparing the available alternatives across pertinent criteria.

### **3.1.3.1 Password Hashing Using Bcrypt** 

A crucial security precaution is password hashing, which transforms plaintext passwords into an unchangeable, fixed-length string of characters. Hashed passwords are much safer to store in a database than encryption, since they cannot be decrypted (Grassi et al., 2017). Because of its design features, which are especially well suited for password security in web-based applications, bcrypt was chosen for Katuwang among the available hashing algorithms.

By adding a randomly generated salt to each password prior to hashing, bcrypt is an adaptive hashing function that stops attackers from cracking stored passwords using precomputed hash tables, also referred to as rainbow tables. More significantly, bcrypt has an adjustable cost factor that regulates the computational cost of the hashing procedure. Because the cost factor can be raised to prevent brute-force attacks, bcrypt is intrinsically future-resistant by design (Grassi et al., 2017). According to Aumasson (2018), bcrypt is far more resistant to contemporary attack vectors than general-purpose hashing functions because of its intentional slowness and salting mechanism. 

Faster hashing algorithms like MD5 and SHA-256, on the other hand, were created for data integrity verification and speed rather than password storage. Because of their computational efficiency, they are susceptible to dictionary and brute-force attacks, because modern hardware allows an attacker to try billions of guesses every second (Grassi et al., 2017). Although SHA-3 is more secure than MD5 and SHA-256, it still lacks the native adaptive cost factor and built-in salting that bcrypt offers (Dworkin et al., 2015). The 2015 Password Hashing Competition winner, Argon2, is currently regarded as the most theoretically sound choice and provides memory-hard defense against GPU-based attacks (Biryukov et al., 2016); however, for a school-based platform functioning at the scale of TRIS, it necessitates extra configuration and dependency management, which increases implementation complexity without offering much practical benefit. The comparison of these hashing algorithms is summed up in Table 4\.

# 

**Table 4:** *Comparison of Password Hashing Algorithms*

| Algorithm | Built-in Salting | Adaptive Cost Factor | Hashing Speed | Brute force Resistance | Implementation Complexity | Suitability for Password Storage |
| ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| MD5 | No | No | Very fast | Very low | Low | Not recommended |
| SHA-256 | No | No | Very fast | Low | Low | Not recommended |
| SHA-3 | No | No | Fast | Moderate | Low | Acceptable with manual salting |
| **Bcrypt (selected)** | **Yes** | **Yes** | **Intentionally slow** | **High** | **Low–Moderate** | **Highly recommended** |
| Argon2 | Yes | Yes | Intentionally slow | Very high | Moderate-High | Highly recommended |

As demonstrated in Table 4, bcrypt continues to be the industry standard for web applications because of its native salting, configurable cost factor, broad library support, and low implementation complexity, all of which are in line with Katuwang's technical scope and resource constraints, despite Argon2's potential advantage in fending off GPU-based attacks.

### **3.1.3.2 Role-Based Access Control for User Permissions** 

A security model called Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) limits system access according to the roles that each user is assigned. Rather than giving each user direct access to permissions, RBAC links permissions to roles. Users are then given a role that matches their role in the system. This lowers the possibility of illegal access, data breaches, and unintentional abuse of system functions by guaranteeing that users can only access features and information pertinent to their duties (Hu et al., 2018).

There are three different user roles in Katuwang: Administrator, Student Tutor, and Student Learner. Depending on its purpose, each role has a unique set of permissions. For instance, student learners are able to take tests and request tutoring, but they are unable to view user profiles or access the analytics dashboard. Although they can access all system management tools, administrators are unable to change the session records of other users without the required authorization. RBAC successfully operationalizes this fundamental tenet of secure system design in multi-user web applications, the principle of least privilege, according to which each user is given only the minimal access required to carry out their role (Servos and Osborn, 2017). 

Because RBAC offers a framework for a multi-role educational platform that is organized, controllable, and auditable, it was chosen over other access control models. Since permissions must be set up per user per resource, the Discretionary Access Control (DAC) and Access Control List (ACL) models become unmanageable as the number of users increases (Servos and Osborn, 2017). Despite being extremely secure, Mandatory Access Control (MAC) is too strict for a voluntary school-based platform and is intended for classified government or military settings (Santos et al., 2020). Although powerful and adaptable, Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) adds needless policy complexity to a system with just three distinct user types. RBAC is easy to implement with NextAuth.JS; session tokens are simple to audit and correspond directly to Katuwang's three pre-established user roles. The comparison of these access control models is compiled in Table 5\.

**Table 5:** *Comparison of Access Control Models*

| Access Control Model | Permission Assignment | Scalability | Ease of Management | Auditability | Suitable for Multi-role Systems | Suitability for Katuwang |
| ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Discretionary Access Control (DAC) | Owner-defined per resource | Low | Difficult | Low | No | Not suitable |
| Mandatory Access Control (MAC) | System-enforced security labels | Moderate | Complex | High | Partially | Not suitable — overly rigid |
| Access Control List (ACL) | Per-user, per-resource rules | Low | Difficult at scale | Moderate | No | Not suitable |
| Attribute-Based Access Control (ABAC) | Policies based on user/resource attributes | High | Complex | High | Yes | Overkill for current scope |
| **Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) (selected)** | **Permissions assigned to roles; users assigned to roles** | **High** | **Simple** | **High** | **Yes** | **Highly suitable** |

# 

Given its simplicity, scalability, and direct alignment with the platform's well-defined user role structure, RBAC is the best access control model for Katuwang, as Table 5 illustrates. 

### **3.1.3.3 Weighted Scoring Algorithm for Tutor Matching** 

A weighted scoring algorithm is a decision-making method that evaluates multiple criteria simultaneously by assigning a numerical weight to each criterion based on its relative importance, computing a score for each candidate against those criteria, and selecting the candidate with the highest total weighted score (Zavadskas et al., 2016). In the context of tutor matching, this approach ensures that the most relevant factors such as whether the tutor has demonstrated proficiency in the subject the learner needs help with, whether the tutor and learner are at compatible grade levels, and whether both parties are available at the same time are all considered together rather than in isolation, producing a match that is both academically appropriate and logistically feasible. 

Since the weighted scoring algorithm is transparent, adaptable, and computationally light, it was chosen over alternative matching techniques. In contrast to black-box machine learning models, which necessitate substantial computational resources and large training datasets (Jordan and Mitchell, 2015), a weighted scoring algorithm uses pre-established, human-interpretable rules that administrators can modify as TRIS requirements change. Weighted scoring creates an ordered list of candidates so that the best match is always given priority, in contrast to straightforward rule-based filtering that just removes unqualified tutors without ranking them (Zavadskas et al., 2016). Although collaborative filtering works well in large-scale recommendation systems, it needs a lot of historical interaction data to produce accurate recommendations; data that a recently implemented platform that serves a single school does not yet have (Zhang et al., 2019). Constraint-based matching produces matches that are technically valid but may not be ideal, because it assesses hard requirements without having the flexibility to prioritize some criteria over others (Zhang et al., 2019). All of these drawbacks are addressed by the weighted scoring algorithm, which evaluates all pertinent criteria concurrently, generates a ranked list of qualified tutors, doesn't require training data, is computationally light enough to operate on modest school infrastructure, and lets administrators modify the weight of each criterion as the program develops. The comparison of these matching approaches is summarized in Table 6\.

### 

**Table 6:** *Comparisons of Matching Approaches*

| Approach | Multi-Criteria | Ranked Results | Transparency | Training Data Required | Computational Load | Reconfigurable | Suitability for Katuwang |
| ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| Manual Matching (Admin-assigned) | Partially | No | High | No | None | Yes | Not scalable |
| Simple Rule-Based Filtering | Partially | No | High | No | Very low | Yes | Acceptable but limited |
| Collaborative Filtering | Yes | Yes | Low | Yes, large dataset | High | No | Not suitable  insufficient data |
| Machine Learning-Based Matching | Yes | Yes | Low | Yes, extensive dataset | High | No | Not suitable  overkill for scope |
| Constraint-Based Matching | Yes | Yes | High | No | Low | Yes | Acceptable but inflexible |
| **Weighted Scoring Algorithm (selected)** | **Yes** | **Yes** | **High** | **No** | **Very low** | **Yes** | **Highly suitable** |

Given its transparency, adaptability, low computational requirements, and compatibility with the operational limitations of a recently implemented school-based platform, the weighted scoring algorithm is the best matching strategy for Katuwang, as indicated in Table 6\. 

### **3.1.3.4 Displayed Name Generation Policy** 

To protect the identity of student users while maintaining a structured and traceable platform environment, Katuwang will implement an automatic displayed name generation system for all registered users. Rather than allowing users to freely select their own displayed names  which introduces risks of impersonation, inappropriate content, and identity duplication. The platform will assign a system-generated unique identifier to each user upon successful registration. This will align with the platform's data minimization principle under the Data Privacy Act of 2012 (Republic Act No. 10173), ensuring that no personally identifiable information such as a student's real name or school ID number will be exposed within the platform interface. 

The generated identifier will follow a role-based prefix format, where Student Tutors will be assigned identifiers following the format TUT-XXXX and Student Learners will be assigned identifiers following the format STU-XXXX, with XXXX representing a four-digit zero-padded sequential number. The first registered tutor will receive TUT-0001, the second TUT-0002, and so on, independently from the learner sequence which will begin at STU-0001. This format will provide immediate role recognition without disclosing any personal information about the user. 

This identifier will be generated through a simple sequential counter algorithm which would be unique for each registered user. Upon the success of registering a new user, the system would deduce the users role and append the appropriate prefix, also consulting another sequence counter table. This table would contain the last number assigned to each prefix independently. This value will be incremented, padded with leading zero until the length of the number is four, concatenated with the prefix and then checked for uniqueness against the users table. If the identifier already exists then the process will be repeated by incrementing the counter and continuing until a unique identifier is found and assigned to the user profile. 

# **3.2 Software Methodology**

The researchers will adopt the Agile Software Development Life Cycle (SDLC) using the Scrum framework to guide the development of Katuwang. Agile Scrum was selected for its flexibility, iterative approach, and strong emphasis on continuous feedback. According to Schwaber and Sutherland (2020) in the Official Scrum Guide. Scrum is a lightweight framework designed to help teams generate value through adaptive solutions for complex problems, making it especially suitable for projects whose requirements evolve through stakeholder engagement. This flexibility allows the research team to refine and improve the platform at any stage before final delivery, which is particularly important for Katuwang, as the specific academic support needs of Taysan Resettlement Integrated School can only be fully understood through ongoing consultations with the school principal, selected faculty, and selected students. Furthermore, Agile's iterative structure, organized into short development cycles or sprints, enables the team to deliver functional system increments regularly rather than waiting for full completion before presenting results to stakeholders (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). This approach allows the principal and selected faculty of TRIS to review progress and provide timely feedback, ensuring that the platform consistently reflects the actual academic needs of the school community.

The Agile Scrum process followed in this study is illustrated in Figure 2, which depicts the cyclical flow from Requirements Planning and User Design through the Product Backlog, Sprint Planning, Daily Scrum, Sprint Release, Retrospective, and Refinement, all supported by continuous feedback loops from the school principal, selected faculty, and selected students of Taysan Resettlement Integrated School.  

***Figure 2\.** Agile Process Flow (Scrum.org, 2020\)*

### **3.2.1 Requirements Planning**

The first phase of the development process will focus on the systematic gathering, analysis, and documentation of the system requirements that Katuwang must fulfill. This phase will combine a review of related literature and existing peer tutoring platforms with direct stakeholder consultation at TRIS, including the school principal, selected faculty members, and selected students. Data will be collected through semi-structured interviews and a needs assessment survey, ensuring that the identified requirements accurately reflect the actual academic support workflow of the school, including the voluntary and free-of-charge nature of the peer tutoring services and the specific subject areas and grade levels that require intervention. All gathered requirements will be organized into a structured list of system features and constraints, clearly defining the expected functionalities for each user role: Student Learner, Student Tutor, Teacher Moderator, and Administrator. This list will serve as the foundation for the Product Backlog and will guide the development of all six core modules of Katuwang.

**3.2.2 User Design**  
After establishing system requirements, the study will proceed to the User Design phase, which focuses on creating prototypes and visual representations of Katuwang's interface. This phase aligns with Agile principles that promote iterative development and continuous stakeholder feedback through working prototypes (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020), and will function as an essential bridge between requirements planning and actual system development. During this phase, the research team will design wireframes and high-fidelity UI/UX prototypes for all major system components, including the student tutoring request and tutor profile browsing pages, the tutor session management and progress tracking interfaces, the chatbot assistant module, and the administrator analytics dashboard.  These prototypes will be presented to the principal and selected faculty of TRIS for evaluation, focusing on usability, navigation flow, visual clarity, and alignment with the school's operational needs. Feedback gathered during this stage will guide design refinements before implementation begins.  The finalized design will serve as the official blueprint for development, ensuring that the system is intentionally shaped around the needs of TRIS students rather than being improvised during coding.

### **3.2.3 Product Backlog**

Following the design phase, all identified system features and requirements will be organized into a structured Product Backlog. Each Product Backlog Item (PBI) will include a brief description of the task, specify the team members to whom it is assigned, and indicate its priority and estimated timeline (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). The backlog for Katuwang will be arranged according to system importance, with core and high-impact features such as the tutor-learner matching algorithm, the session management module, the user authentication and role-based access control system, and the pre- and post-test assessment module, positioned at the top of the backlog and addressed first during sprint execution. Each PBI will be directly linked to specific system objectives, maintaining alignment between the study's goals and implementation tasks. As development progresses and feedback is gathered from TRIS stakeholders, the backlog will be continuously updated to reflect changing priorities and newly identified requirements, ensuring that the most critical features are consistently developed ahead of lower-priority enhancements.

### **3.2.4 Sprint Planning**

With the product backlog established, sprint planning sessions will be conducted to determine which tasks will be completed in each development cycle. Agile project planning organizes work into iterative units called sprints, wherein the Scrum Master identifies the product backlog items for the upcoming cycle, logs them on the scrum board, and sets the sprint duration based on resource availability and team capacity (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). In this study, the research team leader will assume the role of the Scrum Master, responsible for organizing sprint planning sessions, distributing tasks among team members, and maintaining the scrum board. Each sprint will focus on a specific set of related modules or features, and its duration will be determined by task complexity and the team's available working hours within their academic schedule.

### **3.2.5 Daily Scrum**

During each sprint, the team will hold regular Daily Scrum meetings to monitor progress and address any challenges encountered. These short, structured meetings promote transparency, coordination, and the timely resolution of issues by reviewing ongoing tasks and identifying obstacles (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). The Scrum Master will oversee task progress using a digital scrum board, which visually tracks the status of all development activities and ensures that tasks remain aligned with the sprint timeline. Any technical or implementation issues encountered during the development of Katuwang's modules such as difficulties in implementing the weighted scoring algorithm for tutor-learner matching or configuring the intent-based chatbot logic, will be discussed during these sessions and resolved promptly. This continuous communication promotes accountability, minimizes delays, and ensures that all team members remain coordinated throughout each sprint.

### 

### 

### **3.2.6 Sprint Release**

At the end of each sprint, a Sprint Release will be conducted, wherein the completed system features are presented to the principal and selected faculty of TRIS for review. This allows stakeholders to interact with a working increment of the platform and evaluate its effectiveness in addressing the school's academic support needs. Feedback gathered during this stage will be documented and used to guide subsequent improvements. This regular delivery of functional system increments to the end-users is a core principle of Agile, as it enables continuous stakeholder involvement and ensures that development remains aligned with the actual needs of TRIS students and faculty rather than assumptions made at the outset of the project (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020).

### **3.2.7 Retrospective**

After each sprint release, the team will conduct a Retrospective meeting to evaluate the completed sprint before proceeding to the next development cycle. This session provides an opportunity to reflect on what was accomplished, identify challenges encountered during development, and determine areas for process improvement (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). The team will discuss what worked well, what issues arose, and how workflows can be refined in subsequent sprints. Feedback from TRIS stakeholders gathered during the sprint release will also be reviewed during this session to identify necessary adjustments or enhancements to the platform. Insights gained from the retrospective will directly inform the planning and execution of the following sprint, supporting continuous improvement throughout the project lifecycle.

### **3.2.8 Refinement**

The final recurring phase in each sprint cycle is Refinement, during which the Product Backlog will be reviewed and updated based on the outcomes of the recently completed sprint and feedback from stakeholders. This process ensures that all remaining tasks remain relevant, clearly defined, and accurately estimated (Schwaber & Sutherland, 2020). During refinement sessions, the team will revisit backlog items scheduled for upcoming sprints to incorporate any new requirements or modifications surfaced during the sprint release and retrospective. Any requested changes from the principal and selected faculty of TRIS such as additions to the chatbot knowledge base, adjustments to the tutor assessment criteria, or modifications to the analytics dashboard reports, will be formally documented and integrated into the backlog during this phase. This continuous updating process keeps the backlog accurate and ensures that the development plan remains aligned with TRIS's evolving needs and the study's objectives across all remaining development cycles.

# **3.3 Evaluation procedure**

The system will be evaluated based on the ISO/IEC 25010\. This evaluation mechanism is designed to assess how effectively the Katuwang platform addresses academic support gaps at Taysan Resettlement Integrated School (TRIS). A structured survey questionnaire will be distributed to the four primary user groups, Student Learners, Student Tutors, Teacher Moderators, and Administrators, following a supervised hands-on orientation and walkthrough of the platform. This ensures that all respondents provide feedback grounded in direct experience with the system's core features, such as tutor matching and the chatbot assistant. The evaluation results will be statistically analyzed using a 5-point Likert scale to determine the overall effectiveness and quality of the platform as shown in Table 4\. The Weighted Mean (WM) will be computed for each quality dimension and interpreted using the following scale:

***Table 7\.** Likert Scale Evaluation*

| Range | Rating | Description |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 5 | Strongly Agree | The system demonstrates very high compliance with Functional Suitability, Performance Efficiency, Interaction Capability, Reliability, Flexibility, and Security.  |
| 4 | Agree | The system demonstrates high compliance with the evaluated ISO/IEC 25010 attributes.  |
| 3 | Neutral | The system demonstrates moderate compliance with the evaluated attributes.  |
| 2 | Disagree | The system demonstrates low compliance with the evaluated attributes.  |
| 1 | Strongly Disagree  | The system demonstrates very low or no compliance with the evaluated attributes.  |

# **Sprint Plan**

**8\. 16 Week Sprint Plan**

| Sprint No | Focus | Maps to Study Objective |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 0 | Requirements, Design, Backlog | Requirements gathering phase |
| 1 | User Mgmt / Auth / RBAC (verify \+ harden) | Core module — user management |
| 2 | Session Management | Core module — session management |
| 3 | Tutor Matching (weighted scoring) | Core module — matching algorithm |
| 4 | Assessment (cert \+ pre/post-test) | Core module — assessment |
| 5 | Chatbot Assistant | Core module — chatbot |
| 6 | Analytics Dashboard | Core module — analytics |
| 7 | Integration, testing, UAT | System validation |
| 8 | ISO 25010 evaluation, deployment, defense prep | Study evaluation phase |

# **2nd Week Implementation Status**

| Sprint | Feature | Status | Report |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | ----- |
| 1 | Database Schema & Migrations | Partial | Full MariaDB schema with Prisma ORM, relations, and unique constraints. |
| 1 | Database Seeding (seed.ts) | Partial | Initial baseline seed for IdCounter table (TUTOR & LEARNER). |
| 1 | Bcrypt Authentication & NextAuth | Partial | NextAuth credentials provider, password hashing, JWT sessions. |
| 1 | Atomic Anonymous ID Generation | Partial | Transactional atomic counters producing STU-0001 & TUT-0001. |
| 1 | Learner Registration & Validation | Partial | Full Zod schema validation, data privacy consent, form UI. |
| 1 | Tutor Registration & Validation | Partial | Profile initialization, subject application records, form UI. |
| 1 | Route Protection & RBAC Proxy | Partial | Server-side role validation on all portals and API routes. |
| 2 | Tutor Class Management UI | Pending | Tabbed interface, scheduling modal, inspector modal, delete/complete actions. |
| 2 | Tutor Class Creation API | Pending | Zod validation, certification enforcement, past-date & overlap protection. |
| 2 | Tutor Class Update & Delete API | Pending | Partial updates, enrollment-based capacity restrictions, safe-delete checks. |
| 2 | Learner Portal UI Baseline | Pending | Dashboard layout with anonymous ID badge and quick links. |
| 2 | Admin Portal Baselines | Pending | Secured role-based views with user greetings and signout. |
| 2 | Learner Class Browsing & Enrollment | Pending | Enrollment model ready; learner discovery and booking UI pending. |
| 2 | One-on-One / Group Setup Selection | Pending | Learner-facing option when submitting a session request. |
| 2 | Session Status Lifecycle | Pending | SCHEDULED → COMPLETED / CANCELLED transitions and guards. |
| 3 | Weighted Scoring Algorithm | Pending | Subject expertise \+ grade-level compatibility \+ tutor availability. |
| 3 | Learner Session/Topic Request Form | Pending | Captures academic level, subject, preferred schedule. |
| 3 | Matching Engine API | Pending | Returns ranked tutor recommendations from weighted score. |
| 3 | "No Match Found" Fallback Flow | Pending | Learner submits open tutor request (subject, topic, availability, setup preference). |
| 3 | Tutor Availability Model | Pending | Schedule data structure feeding the scoring engine. |
| 3 | Match Result UI | Pending | Learner-facing recommended tutor card(s). |
| 4 | Diagnostic Assessment Engine | Pending | Stub models created (Assessment); interactive quiz UI pending. |
| 4 | Tutor Certification Exam | Pending | Qualifies tutors before matching eligibility; drives PENDING → PARTIAL/CERTIFIED/REJECTED. |
| 4 | Pre-Test Engine | Pending | Administered before each tutoring session. |
| 4 | Post-Test Engine | Pending | Administered after each session; scored against pre-test. |
| 4 | Assessment Data Model | Pending | Links results to Session, TutorProfile, and learner progress. |
| 4 | Certification Status Gating | Pending | Uncertified tutors excluded from the matching pool. |
| 4 | Learner Progress Reporting | Pending | Before/after score comparison view. |
| 5 | Intent-Based Response Logic | Pending | Rule/pattern matching for identifying user requests. |
| 5 | Navigation Help Intents | Pending | Guides users through platform sections. |
| 5 | FAQ Knowledge Base | Pending | Predefined answers sourced from stakeholder interviews. |
| 5 | Session Recommendation Intent | Pending | Suggests tutoring sessions based on learner profile/needs. |
| 5 | Chat UI Widget | Pending | Embedded assistant accessible from learner/tutor portals. |
| 6 | Admin Analytics Dashboard | Pending | System administrator dashboard scaffolded; charts/reports pending. |
| 6 | Session Participation Visualizations | Pending | Graphs/charts only — no predictive or ML-based analytics, per study scope. |
| 6 | Tutor Performance Overview | Pending | Certification stats, session counts per tutor. |
| 6 | System Usage Statistics | Pending | Registration counts, active sessions, module usage. |
| 6 | Privacy-Safe Reporting | Pending | Aggregate-only views; no PII beyond anonymous IDs. |
| 6 | Moderator Portal Baseline | Pending | Secured role-based view with greeting/signout scaffolded. |
| 7 | End-to-End Integration Testing | Pending | Full user journeys across all 6 modules per role. |
| 7 | Security Review | Pending | RBAC boundary testing, bcrypt config audit, Zod coverage audit. |
| 7 | UAT with TRIS Stakeholders | Pending | Principal, faculty, and selected students test the live system. |
| 8 | ISO/IEC 25010 Evaluation Survey | Pending | 5-point Likert survey across Learners, Tutors, Moderators, Admins. |
| 8 | Final Deployment | Pending | Production build delivered to TRIS hosting environment. |
| 8 | System Documentation | Pending | User manuals, API reference, defense materials. |

# **1\. Status Legend**

| Status | Definition |
| :---- | :---- |
| **Not Yet Started** | No work has begun on this feature; it is further down the roadmap and not yet scheduled for the upcoming sprint/week. |
| **Pending** | Next in line — scheduled to begin in the upcoming sprint or week, but work has not started yet. |
| **Partial** | Work is underway; the feature has started but is not yet fully completed. |
| **Not tested** |  |
| **Complete** | Fully implemented and done for the entire feature/module — no remaining work. |
